L At the very basic level, developing using Vite is not that different from using a static file server. However, Vite provides many enhancements over native ESM imports to support various features that are typically seen in bundler-based setups.

L ## Hot Module Replacement
T 2 text.html.markdown markup.heading.markdown heading.2.markdown punctuation.definition.heading.markdown
T 3 text.html.markdown markup.heading.markdown heading.2.markdown
T 25 text.html.markdown markup.heading.markdown heading.2.markdown entity.name.section.markdown
L 
L Vite provides an [HMR API](./api-hmr) over native ESM. Frameworks with HMR capabilities can leverage the API to provide instant, precise updates without reloading the page or blowing away application state. Vite provides first-party HMR integrations for [Vue Single File Components](https://github.com/vitejs/vite-plugin-vue/tree/main/packages/plugin-vue) and [React Fast Refresh](https://github.com/vitejs/vite-plugin-react/tree/main/packages/plugin-react). There are also official integrations for Preact via [@prefresh/vite](https://github.com/JoviDeCroock/prefresh/tree/main/packages/vite).

L ### Transpile Only
T 3 text.html.markdown markup.heading.markdown heading.3.markdown punctuation.definition.heading.markdown
T 4 text.html.markdown markup.heading.markdown heading.3.markdown
T 18 text.html.markdown markup.heading.markdown heading.3.markdown entity.name.section.markdown
L 
L Note that Vite only performs transpilation on `.ts` files and does **NOT** perform type checking. It assumes type checking is taken care of by your IDE and build process.
T 46 text.html.markdown meta.paragraph.markdown
T 47 text.html.markdown meta.paragraph.markdown markup.inline.raw.string.markdown punctuation.definition.raw.markdown
T 50 text.html.markdown meta.paragraph.markdown markup.inline.raw.string.markdown
T 51 text.html.markdown meta.paragraph.markdown markup.inline.raw.string.markdown punctuation.definition.raw.markdown
T 67 text.html.markdown meta.paragraph.markdown
T 69 text.html.markdown meta.paragraph.markdown markup.bold.markdown punctuation.definition.bold.markdown
T 72 text.html.markdown meta.paragraph.markdown markup.bold.markdown
T 74 text.html.markdown meta.paragraph.markdown markup.bold.markdown punctuation.definition.bold.markdown
T 170 text.html.markdown meta.paragraph.markdown
L 
L The reason Vite does not perform type checking as part of the transform process is because the two jobs work fundamentally differently. Transpilation can work on a per-file basis and aligns perfectly with Vite's on-demand compile model. In comparison, type checking requires knowledge of the entire module graph. Shoe-horning type checking into Vite's transform pipeline will inevitably compromise Vite's speed benefits.
T 420 text.html.markdown meta.paragraph.markdown
L 
L Vite's job is to get your source modules into a form that can run in the browser as fast as possible. To that end, we recommend separating static analysis checks from Vite's transform pipeline. This principle applies to other static analysis checks such as ESLint.
T 264 text.html.markdown meta.paragraph.markdown
L 
L - For production builds, you can run `tsc --noEmit` in addition to Vite's build command.

L ### TypeScript Compiler Options
T 3 text.html.markdown markup.heading.markdown heading.3.markdown punctuation.definition.heading.markdown
T 4 text.html.markdown markup.heading.markdown heading.3.markdown
T 31 text.html.markdown markup.heading.markdown heading.3.markdown entity.name.section.markdown
L 
L Vite respects some of the options in `tsconfig.json` and sets the corresponding Oxc Transformer options. By default, Vite uses the closest parent `tsconfig.json` that matches each file. A config referenced by that config's [`references`](https://www.typescriptlang.org/tsconfig/#references) field is used when it matches the file. Vite treats a config as matching the file when the file satisfies the config's [`files`](https://www.typescriptlang.org/tsconfig/#files), [`include`](https://www.typescriptlang.org/tsconfig/#include), and [`exclude`](https://www.typescriptlang.org/tsconfig/#exclude) fields.

L Vite starter templates have `"skipLibCheck": true` by default to avoid typechecking dependencies, as they may choose to only support specific versions and configurations of TypeScript. You can learn more at [vuejs/vue-cli#5688](https://github.com/vuejs/vue-cli/pull/5688).

L Vite improves `@import` resolving for Sass and Less so that Vite aliases are also respected. L ## JSON
T 7 text.html.markdown

L 
L ```js twoslash

L import 'vite/client'
T 20 text.html.markdown
L // ---cut---
T 12 text.html.markdown
L // import the entire object
T 27 text.html.markdown
L import json from './example.json'
T 33 text.html.markdown
L // import a root field as named exports - helps with tree-shaking!
T 66 text.html.markdown
L import { field } from './example.json'
T 38 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown

L ## Glob Import
T 14 text.html.markdown
L 
L Vite supports importing multiple modules from the file system via the special `import.meta.glob` function:
T 106 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```js twoslash
T 14 text.html.markdown
L import 'vite/client'
T 20 text.html.markdown
L // ---cut---
T 12 text.html.markdown
L const modules = import.meta.glob('./dir/*.js')
T 46 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L The above will be transformed into the following:
T 49 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```js
T 5 text.html.markdown
L // code produced by vite
T 24 text.html.markdown
L const modules = {
T 17 text.html.markdown
L   './dir/bar.js': () => import('./dir/bar.js'),
T 47 text.html.markdown
L   './dir/foo.js': () => import('./dir/foo.js'),
T 47 text.html.markdown
L }
T 1 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L You can then iterate over the keys of the `modules` object to access the corresponding modules:
T 95 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```js
T 5 text.html.markdown
L for (const path in modules) {
T 29 text.html.markdown
L   modules[path]().then((mod) => {
T 33 text.html.markdown
L     console.log(path, mod)
T 26 text.html.markdown
L   })
T 4 text.html.markdown
L }
T 1 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L Matched files are by default lazy-loaded via dynamic import and will be split into separate chunks during build. If you'd rather import all the modules directly (e.g. relying on side-effects in these modules to be applied first), you can pass `{ eager: true }` as the second argument:
T 284 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```js twoslash
T 14 text.html.markdown
L import 'vite/client'
T 20 text.html.markdown
L // ---cut---
T 12 text.html.markdown
L const modules = import.meta.glob('./dir/*.js', { eager: true })
T 63 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L The above will be transformed into the following:
T 49 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```js
T 5 text.html.markdown
L // code produced by vite
T 24 text.html.markdown
L import * as __vite_glob_0_0 from './dir/bar.js'
T 47 text.html.markdown
L import * as __vite_glob_0_1 from './dir/foo.js'
T 47 text.html.markdown
L const modules = {
T 17 text.html.markdown
L   './dir/bar.js': __vite_glob_0_0,
T 34 text.html.markdown
L   './dir/foo.js': __vite_glob_0_1,
T 34 text.html.markdown
L }
T 1 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L ### Multiple Patterns
T 21 text.html.markdown
L 
L The first argument can be an array of globs, for example
T 56 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```js twoslash
T 14 text.html.markdown
L import 'vite/client'
T 20 text.html.markdown
L // ---cut---
T 12 text.html.markdown
L const modules = import.meta.glob(['./dir/*.js', './another/*.js'])
T 66 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L ### Negative Patterns
T 21 text.html.markdown
L 
L Negative glob patterns are also supported (prefixed with `!`). To ignore some files from the result, you can add exclude glob patterns to the first argument:
T 157 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```js twoslash
T 14 text.html.markdown
L import 'vite/client'
T 20 text.html.markdown
L // ---cut---
T 12 text.html.markdown
L const modules = import.meta.glob(['./dir/*.js', '!**/bar.js'])
T 62 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```js
T 5 text.html.markdown
L // code produced by vite
T 24 text.html.markdown
L const modules = {
T 17 text.html.markdown
L   './dir/foo.js': () => import('./dir/foo.js'),
T 47 text.html.markdown
L }
T 1 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L #### Named Imports
T 18 text.html.markdown
L 
L It's possible to only import parts of the modules with the `import` options.
T 76 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```ts twoslash
T 14 text.html.markdown
L import 'vite/client'
T 20 text.html.markdown
L // ---cut---
T 12 text.html.markdown
L const modules = import.meta.glob('./dir/*.js', { import: 'setup' })
T 67 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```ts
T 5 text.html.markdown
L // code produced by vite
T 24 text.html.markdown
L const modules = {
T 17 text.html.markdown
L   './dir/bar.js': () => import('./dir/bar.js').then((m) => m.setup),
T 68 text.html.markdown
L   './dir/foo.js': () => import('./dir/foo.js').then((m) => m.setup),
T 68 text.html.markdown
L }
T 1 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L When combined with `eager` it's even possible to have tree-shaking enabled for those modules.
T 93 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```ts twoslash
T 14 text.html.markdown
L import 'vite/client'
T 20 text.html.markdown
L // ---cut---
T 12 text.html.markdown
L const modules = import.meta.glob('./dir/*.js', {
T 48 text.html.markdown
L   import: 'setup',
T 18 text.html.markdown
L   eager: true,
T 14 text.html.markdown
L })
T 2 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```ts
T 5 text.html.markdown
L // code produced by vite:
T 25 text.html.markdown
L import { setup as __vite_glob_0_0 } from './dir/bar.js'
T 55 text.html.markdown
L import { setup as __vite_glob_0_1 } from './dir/foo.js'
T 55 text.html.markdown
L const modules = {
T 17 text.html.markdown
L   './dir/bar.js': __vite_glob_0_0,
T 34 text.html.markdown
L   './dir/foo.js': __vite_glob_0_1,
T 34 text.html.markdown
L }
T 1 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L Set `import` to `default` to import the default export.
T 55 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```ts twoslash
T 14 text.html.markdown
L import 'vite/client'
T 20 text.html.markdown
L // ---cut---
T 12 text.html.markdown
L const modules = import.meta.glob('./dir/*.js', {
T 48 text.html.markdown
L   import: 'default',
T 20 text.html.markdown
L   eager: true,
T 14 text.html.markdown
L })
T 2 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```ts
T 5 text.html.markdown
L // code produced by vite:
T 25 text.html.markdown
L import { default as __vite_glob_0_0 } from './dir/bar.js'
T 57 text.html.markdown
L import { default as __vite_glob_0_1 } from './dir/foo.js'
T 57 text.html.markdown
L const modules = {
T 17 text.html.markdown
L   './dir/bar.js': __vite_glob_0_0,
T 34 text.html.markdown
L   './dir/foo.js': __vite_glob_0_1,
T 34 text.html.markdown
L }
T 1 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown

L #### Custom Queries
T 19 text.html.markdown
L 
L You can also use the `query` option to provide queries to imports, for example, to import assets [as a string](/guide/assets.html#importing-asset-as-string) or [as a url](/guide/assets.html#importing-asset-as-url):
T 214 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```ts twoslash
T 14 text.html.markdown
L import 'vite/client'
T 20 text.html.markdown
L // ---cut---
T 12 text.html.markdown
L const moduleStrings = import.meta.glob('./dir/*.svg', {
T 55 text.html.markdown
L   query: '?raw',
T 16 text.html.markdown
L   import: 'default',
T 20 text.html.markdown
L })
T 2 text.html.markdown
L const moduleUrls = import.meta.glob('./dir/*.svg', {
T 52 text.html.markdown
L   query: '?url',
T 16 text.html.markdown
L   import: 'default',
T 20 text.html.markdown
L })
T 2 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```ts
T 5 text.html.markdown
L // code produced by vite:
T 25 text.html.markdown
L const moduleStrings = {
T 23 text.html.markdown
L   './dir/bar.svg': () => import('./dir/bar.svg?raw').then((m) => m['default']),
T 79 text.html.markdown
L   './dir/foo.svg': () => import('./dir/foo.svg?raw').then((m) => m['default']),
T 79 text.html.markdown
L }
T 1 text.html.markdown
L const moduleUrls = {
T 20 text.html.markdown
L   './dir/bar.svg': () => import('./dir/bar.svg?url').then((m) => m['default']),
T 79 text.html.markdown
L   './dir/foo.svg': () => import('./dir/foo.svg?url').then((m) => m['default']),
T 79 text.html.markdown
L }
T 1 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L You can also provide custom queries for other plugins to consume:
T 65 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```ts twoslash
T 14 text.html.markdown
L import 'vite/client'
T 20 text.html.markdown
L // ---cut---
T 12 text.html.markdown
L const modules = import.meta.glob('./dir/*.js', {
T 48 text.html.markdown
L   query: { foo: 'bar', bar: true },
T 35 text.html.markdown
L })
T 2 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L #### Base Path
T 14 text.html.markdown
L 
L You can also use the `base` option to provide base path for the imports:
T 72 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```ts twoslash
T 14 text.html.markdown
L import 'vite/client'
T 20 text.html.markdown
L // ---cut---
T 12 text.html.markdown
L const modulesWithBase = import.meta.glob('./**/*.js', {
T 55 text.html.markdown
L   base: './base',
T 17 text.html.markdown
L })
T 2 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```ts
T 5 text.html.markdown
L // code produced by vite:
T 25 text.html.markdown
L const modulesWithBase = {
T 25 text.html.markdown
L   './dir/foo.js': () => import('./base/dir/foo.js'),
T 52 text.html.markdown
L   './dir/bar.js': () => import('./base/dir/bar.js'),
T 52 text.html.markdown
L }
T 1 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L The base option can only be a directory path relative to the importer file or absolute against the project root. Aliases and virtual modules aren't supported.
T 158 text.html.markdown
L 
L Only the globs that are relative paths are interpreted as relative to the resolved base.
T 88 text.html.markdown
L 
L All the resulting module keys are modified to be relative to the base if provided.
T 82 text.html.markdown
L 
L #### Case Sensitive Matching
T 28 text.html.markdown
L 
L By default, glob pattern matching is case-sensitive. You can use the `caseSensitive` option to change this behavior:
T 116 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```ts twoslash
T 14 text.html.markdown
L import 'vite/client'
T 20 text.html.markdown
L // ---cut---
T 12 text.html.markdown
L const modules = import.meta.glob('./dir/module*.js', {
T 54 text.html.markdown
L   caseSensitive: false,
T 23 text.html.markdown
L })
T 2 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L With `caseSensitive: false`, the glob will match files regardless of case (e.g., `Module.js`, `module.js`, `MODULE.js` will all be matched by `module*.js`).
T 156 text.html.markdown
L 
L ### Glob Import Caveats
T 23 text.html.markdown
L 
L Note that:
T 10 text.html.markdown
L 
L - This is a Vite-only feature and is not a web or ES standard.
T 62 text.html.markdown
L - The glob patterns are treated like import specifiers: they must be either relative (start with `./`) or absolute (start with `/`, resolved relative to project root) or an alias path (see [`resolve.alias` option](/config/shared-options.md#resolve-alias)).
T 256 text.html.markdown
L - The glob matching is done via [`tinyglobby`](https://github.com/SuperchupuDev/tinyglobby) - check out its documentation for [supported glob patterns](https://superchupu.dev/tinyglobby/comparison).
T 198 text.html.markdown
L - You should also be aware that all the arguments in the `import.meta.glob` must be **passed as literals**. You can NOT use variables or expressions in them.
T 157 text.html.markdown
L 
L ## Dynamic Import
T 17 text.html.markdown
L 
L Similar to [glob import](#glob-import), Vite also supports dynamic import with variables.
T 89 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```ts
T 5 text.html.markdown
L const module = await import(`./dir/${file}.js`)
T 47 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L Note that variables only represent file names one level deep. If `file` is `'foo/bar'`, the import would fail. For more advanced usage, you can use the [glob import](#glob-import) feature.
T 188 text.html.markdown
L 
L Also note that the dynamic import must match the following rules to be bundled:
T 79 text.html.markdown
L 
L - Imports must start with `./` or `../` or `#`: ``import(`./dir/${foo}.js`)`` is valid, but ``import(`${foo}.js`)`` is not.
T 123 text.html.markdown
L - Imports must end with a file extension: ``import(`./dir/${foo}.js`)`` is valid, but ``import(`./dir/${foo}`)`` is not.
T 120 text.html.markdown
L - Imports to the own directory must specify a file name pattern: ``import(`./prefix-${foo}.js`)`` is valid, but ``import(`./${foo}.js`)`` is not.
T 145 text.html.markdown
L 
L These rules are enforced to prevent accidentally importing files that are not intended to be bundled. For example, without these rules, `import(foo)` would bundle everything in the file system.
T 193 text.html.markdown
L 
L ## WebAssembly
T 14 text.html.markdown
L 
L Vite supports importing pre-compiled `.wasm` files in two ways: directly as an [ES module](#esm-integration) when you only need the module's exports, or with [`?init`](#manual-initialization) when you need explicit control over instantiation.
T 242 text.html.markdown
L 
L ### ESM Integration
T 19 text.html.markdown
L 
L A `.wasm` file can be imported directly. Vite reads the module's imports and exports from the binary, instantiates it, and re-exposes its exports as named ES module exports:
T 173 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```js
T 5 text.html.markdown
L import { add } from './add.wasm'
T 32 text.html.markdown
L 
L console.log(add(1, 2)) // 3
T 27 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L If the WebAssembly module declares imports of its own, Vite resolves them from JavaScript modules. Each import's module name is treated as an import specifier (resolved relative to the `.wasm` file) and the requested members are wired into the instance automatically.
T 267 text.html.markdown
L 
L This follows the [WebAssembly/ES Module Integration proposal](https://github.com/WebAssembly/esm-integration). Because a WebAssembly module is instantiated asynchronously, a directly imported `.wasm` file behaves as an async module and requires top-level `await` support.
T 271 text.html.markdown
L 
L ::: tip TypeScript support
T 26 text.html.markdown
L 
L Since the types of `.wasm` files are unknown, TypeScript will report errors like `Module '"*.wasm"' has no exported member 'add'`. To fix this, enable [`allowArbitraryExtensions`](https://www.typescriptlang.org/tsconfig/#allowArbitraryExtensions) in your `tsconfig.json` and create a declaration file next to your `.wasm` file. With `allowArbitraryExtensions` enabled, TypeScript will look for a declaration file named `{filename}.d.wasm.ts` when resolving a `.wasm` import. For example, for `add.wasm`, create `add.d.wasm.ts`:
T 527 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```ts [add.d.wasm.ts]
T 21 text.html.markdown
L export function add(a: number, b: number): number
T 49 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L :::
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L ### Manual Initialization
T 25 text.html.markdown
L 
L When you need control over when and how the module is instantiated, import it with `?init`. The default export will be an initialization function that returns a Promise of the [`WebAssembly.Instance`](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/WebAssembly/JavaScript_interface/Instance):
T 285 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```js twoslash
T 14 text.html.markdown
L import 'vite/client'
T 20 text.html.markdown
L // ---cut---
T 12 text.html.markdown
L import init from './example.wasm?init'
T 38 text.html.markdown
L 
L init().then((instance) => {
T 27 text.html.markdown
L   instance.exports.test()
T 25 text.html.markdown
L })
T 2 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L The init function can also take an importObject which is passed along to [`WebAssembly.instantiate`](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/WebAssembly/JavaScript_interface/instantiate) as its second argument:
T 211 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```js twoslash
T 14 text.html.markdown
L import 'vite/client'
T 20 text.html.markdown
L import init from './example.wasm?init'
T 38 text.html.markdown
L // ---cut---
T 12 text.html.markdown
L init({
T 6 text.html.markdown
L   imports: {
T 12 text.html.markdown
L     someFunc: () => {
T 21 text.html.markdown
L       /* ... */
T 15 text.html.markdown
L     },
T 6 text.html.markdown
L   },
T 4 text.html.markdown
L }).then(() => {
T 15 text.html.markdown
L   /* ... */
T 11 text.html.markdown
L })
T 2 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L In the production build, `.wasm` files smaller than `assetsInlineLimit` will be inlined as base64 strings. Otherwise, they will be treated as a [static asset](./assets) and fetched on-demand.
T 191 text.html.markdown
L 
L ::: warning For SSR build, Node.js compatible runtimes are only supported
T 73 text.html.markdown
L 
L Due to the lack of a universal way to load a file, the internal implementation for both direct `.wasm` imports and `.wasm?init` relies on the `node:fs` module. This means that these features will only work in Node.js compatible runtimes for SSR builds.
T 252 text.html.markdown
L 
L :::
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L ### Accessing the WebAssembly Module
T 36 text.html.markdown
L 
L If you need access to the `Module` object, e.g. to instantiate it multiple times, use an [explicit URL import](./assets#explicit-url-imports) to resolve the asset, and then perform the instantiation:
T 199 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```js twoslash
T 14 text.html.markdown
L import 'vite/client'
T 20 text.html.markdown
L // ---cut---
T 12 text.html.markdown
L import wasmUrl from 'foo.wasm?url'
T 34 text.html.markdown
L 
L const main = async () => {
T 26 text.html.markdown
L   const responsePromise = fetch(wasmUrl)
T 40 text.html.markdown
L   const { module, instance } =
T 30 text.html.markdown
L     await WebAssembly.instantiateStreaming(responsePromise)
T 59 text.html.markdown
L   /* ... */
T 11 text.html.markdown
L }
T 1 text.html.markdown
L 
L main()
T 6 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown

L ## Web Workers
T 14 text.html.markdown
L 
L ### Import with Constructors
T 28 text.html.markdown
L 
L A web worker script can be imported using [`new Worker()`](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Worker/Worker) and [`new SharedWorker()`](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/SharedWorker/SharedWorker). Compared to the worker suffixes, this syntax leans closer to the standards and is the **recommended** way to create workers.
T 351 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```ts
T 5 text.html.markdown
L const worker = new Worker(new URL('./worker.js', import.meta.url))
T 66 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L The worker constructor also accepts options, which can be used to create "module" workers:
T 90 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```ts
T 5 text.html.markdown
L const worker = new Worker(new URL('./worker.js', import.meta.url), {
T 68 text.html.markdown
L   type: 'module',
T 17 text.html.markdown
L })
T 2 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L The worker detection will only work if the `new URL()` constructor is used directly inside the `new Worker()` declaration. Otherwise it is handled as a [static asset URL](./assets#new-url-url-import-meta-url) instead. Additionally, all options parameters must be static values (i.e. string literals).
T 300 text.html.markdown
L 
L ### Import with Query Suffixes
T 30 text.html.markdown
L 
L A web worker script can be directly imported by appending `?worker` or `?sharedworker` to the import request. The default export will be a custom worker constructor:
T 165 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```js twoslash
T 14 text.html.markdown
L import 'vite/client'
T 20 text.html.markdown
L // ---cut---
T 12 text.html.markdown
L import MyWorker from './worker?worker'
T 38 text.html.markdown
L 
L const worker = new MyWorker()
T 29 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L The worker script can also use ESM `import` statements instead of `importScripts()`. **Note**: During development this relies on [browser native support](https://caniuse.com/?search=module%20worker), but for the production build it is compiled away.
T 249 text.html.markdown
L 
L By default, the worker script will be emitted as a separate chunk in the production build. If you wish to inline the worker as base64 strings, add the `inline` query:
T 166 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```js twoslash
T 14 text.html.markdown
L import 'vite/client'
T 20 text.html.markdown
L // ---cut---
T 12 text.html.markdown
L import MyWorker from './worker?worker&inline'
T 45 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L If you wish to retrieve the worker as a URL, add the `url` query:
T 65 text.html.markdown
L 
L ```js twoslash
T 14 text.html.markdown
L import 'vite/client'
T 20 text.html.markdown
L // ---cut---
T 12 text.html.markdown
L import MyWorker from './worker?worker&url'
T 42 text.html.markdown
L ```
T 3 text.html.markdown
L 
L See [Worker Options](/config/worker-options.md) for details on configuring the bundling of all workers.
T 103 text.html.markdown
L 
L ## Content Security Policy (CSP)
T 32 text.html.markdown
L 
L To deploy CSP, certain directives or configs must be set due to Vite's internals.
T 81 text.html.markdown
L 
L ### [`'nonce-{RANDOM}'`](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/HTTP/Headers/Content-Security-Policy/Sources#nonce-base64-value)
T 134 text.html.markdown
L 
L When [`html.cspNonce`](/config/shared-options#html-cspnonce) is set, Vite adds a nonce attribute with the specified value to any `<script>` and `<style>` tags, as well as `<link>` tags for stylesheets and module preloading. Additionally, when this option is set, Vite will inject a meta tag (`<meta property="csp-nonce" nonce="PLACEHOLDER" />`).

L 
L Vite automatically extracts the CSS used by modules in an async chunk and generates a separate file for it. The CSS file is automatically loaded via a `<link>` tag when the associated async chunk is loaded, and the async chunk is guaranteed to only be evaluated after the CSS is loaded to avoid [FOUC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flash_of_unstyled_content#:~:text=A%20flash%20of%20unstyled%20content,before%20all%20information%20is%20retrieved.).

L 
L If `C` is updated, the only chunk that inherently needs to be invalidated is `C`. However, if `A` references `C` via a normal URL in a static import (i.e. the hash of `C` is included in the URL), the content of `A` is changed, thus `A` would also need to be invalidated. The same applies to `Entry`.

L 
L By utilizing the import maps feature, this issue can be avoided. When this optimization is enabled, Vite will create an import map that maps each chunk's ID to its URL and uses the chunk ID in the import statements instead of the URL. This way, when a chunk is updated, only the updated chunk needs to be invalidated, while the chunks that reference it will not be invalidated.
